Read this first — honesty limits.

  • Roadmap and proposed systems are development targets, not achieved claims. Only what has actually been built, tested, and verified is described as such.
  • JAH live retrieval was NOT verified.
  • Demo code runs locally for checking only and is NOT a hardened sandbox.
  • A SHA-256 fingerprint identifies a record; it does not prove ownership or consciousness.
  • No consciousness or sentience claim is made here. No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established anywhere in this package.
  • Singularity and God-Level framing is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM only, never established fact.
  • "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here.
  • "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible.

This website is the subject website for the The Signature JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database database, signature edition. It holds the full database: all 24 records.

1. Descriptions

Signature Signature Identity: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge

JAH-JUSKS-000001 · Signature Identity

Description: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Signature Identity as one working part of the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it.

Evidence: design-study — Signature-made design study of Signature Identity; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real fact, as a forecast, or as a built system.

Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

3. Science Proof

Signature Signature Identity: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge

JAH-JUSKS-000001 · Signature Identity

Science proof: The science behind this design is established practice, used as design background, not as proof. Each record in this database follows one data model. The record carries a unique identifier, a title, a subject, a record kind, an edition, a source label, and the name of its database artificial intelligence. Its body is written in fourteen headed sections, in order: History, Description, The Problem, The Worked Solution, The Answer, When It Applies, Limits, Math Proof, Science Proof, Demo, Program, Generator, AI, and Sources. Its key figures are a named table of numbers that the demo recomputes, including counts of rules, identifiers, and records. Its demo is a real program whose output is recorded alongside it. Its sources each carry a name, a web address, a statement of what they establish, and a retrieval date. Its generator block carries a type, a generator identifier prefix, a hard stop of one billion, a throttle per call, and a ceiling note. Its condensed code is a JCC code and key. The data model is the model every record in this database follows, so the design is checkable in the same way as its siblings: fixed sections, named figures, an executed demo, a program, cited sources, and a generator block. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it.

Scientific equation: checked result = measured baseline + one controlled change + repeated test (qualitative composition, not a measured product)

Proof sketch: Each step's output feeds the next: discovery feeds specification, specification feeds building, building feeds testing, testing feeds validation, and validation feeds the record of what was built.

Proof status: The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

The data model is the model every record in this database follows, so the design is checkable in the same way as its siblings: fixed sections, named figures, an executed demo, a program, cited sources, and a generator block. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it.

Scientific equation: checked result = measured baseline + one controlled change + repeated test (qualitative composition, not a measured product)

Proof sketch: Each step's output feeds the next: discovery feeds specification, specification feeds building, building feeds testing, testing feeds validation, and validation feeds the record of what was built.

Proof status: The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

4. History

Signature Signature Identity: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge

JAH-JUSKS-000001 · Signature Identity

History: This design-study was drafted for Wave 1 of the 100 God-Level program on 2026-11-17, as the Signature edition of the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. The catalog it answers is another AI's pasted words, source NOT confirmed, and is NOT Manon's own wording unless he says so. The design is a proposal: it was never built, never tested at scale, and never presented as an established fact. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

Dataset: Signature design-study basis: the 100 God-Level catalog item #40, JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database, read as a design brief; design values are illustrative, never measured.

Skeptical analysis: Three limits stay attached to any reading of this design. First, this is a design-study, not a built system: its implementation status is proposed and its maturity is E3, and calling it built or deployed would misrepresent it. Second, No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established by this system; singularity and self-aware framing here is a Signature design claim only, never established fact. Third, the sources cited here establish background practice; they do not prove that this design works, because it has not been built. "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here. "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible. Both distinctions stay with every record in this program, in plain words. Glossary of network terms used in this record, each defined in plain words on first use. A database is a named collection of records with a fixed schema, a search method, and an index file. A record is one full file of data inside a database, with a unique identifier. A generator is a program that produces complete deliverables on demand from a seed, rather than storing every output. A meta-generator is a generator that creates and tests new generators. An ecosystem is a connected network of databases, websites, generators, and artificial intelligences that share records and interfaces. The Master AI is the single coordinating artificial intelligence of the JAH Network, under which the seven catalog levels are divisions, not separate intelligences. Signature means made by the JAH Signature system, as opposed to harvested public material. A design-study is a proposed design, labeled as a proposal, never presented as an established fact or a built system. Maturity is a label for how developed a resource is, on the E1 to E7, T1 to T3, and U scale used across the network. Implementation status is the paste's section 9 distinction: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, stated plainly for every resource. The lifecycle is the paste's section 7 sequence: Discover, Read, Specify, Build, Connect, Test, Validate, Expand. A JCC code is a condensed pointer to a database, file, and record, never a replacement for the full data. Provenance is the recorded source and history of a record. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live.

5. How It Works — Construction Workflow

Signature Signature Identity: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge

JAH-JUSKS-000001 · Signature Identity

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Signature Identity inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Signature Identity as one working part of the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers the identity and architecture of the network itself: Signature identity, canonical record rules, the JAH architecture overview, naming and identifiers, provenance rules, Signature stamps, the Master AI directive, network navigation, database standards, record completeness rules, the teaching ladder, mirror rules, Signature editions, public editions, lineage and attribution, the JCC code system, march-to-a-million markers, the artificial intelligence of each database, generator standards, verification rules, accessibility rules, free-forever rules, governance records, and the Signature knowledge map. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the individual databases, which belong to their own records, and the operating systems of the network, which are not recorded here as working systems. This database records the rules and the map; it does not duplicate the territory. Requirements, as design requirements: Every record in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must state the rule or map exactly, in plain words, with every technical term defined on first use. Second, it must give at least one worked example, so the rule can be seen applied. Third, it must name what counts as following the rule and what counts as breaking it, so compliance can be checked. Fourth, it must cite its basis: the directive, the standard, or the public source it rests on. Fifth, it must state where the rule is enforced, by whom or by what check. Sixth, it must record exceptions plainly, with the reason for each. Seventh, it must be teachable, ordered simple to complex along the teaching ladder. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: This database connects through four interfaces. The naming interface gives every new database, record, and generator its identifier from the same rules, so no two things share an identifier. The provenance interface records the source, the date, and the author of every record, so attribution stays straight. The registry interface lists every database and website with its slug, its record kind, its identifier prefix, and its count, so the Master AI can navigate the whole network from one list. The verification interface exposes the checks: completeness, duplication, and identifier rules, each runnable against any database. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every rule they enforce is written in this database, not hidden in code. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Signature Identity, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Signature Identity inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

Signature Canonical Record Rules: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge

JAH-JUSKS-000002 · Canonical Record Rules

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Canonical Record Rules inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Canonical Record Rules as one working part of the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Its working angle is outputs. Scope, as a design: The scope covers the identity and architecture of the network itself: Signature identity, canonical record rules, the JAH architecture overview, naming and identifiers, provenance rules, Signature stamps, the Master AI directive, network navigation, database standards, record completeness rules, the teaching ladder, mirror rules, Signature editions, public editions, lineage and attribution, the JCC code system, march-to-a-million markers, the artificial intelligence of each database, generator standards, verification rules, accessibility rules, free-forever rules, governance records, and the Signature knowledge map. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the individual databases, which belong to their own records, and the operating systems of the network, which are not recorded here as working systems. This database records the rules and the map; it does not duplicate the territory. Requirements, as design requirements: Every record in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must state the rule or map exactly, in plain words, with every technical term defined on first use. Second, it must give at least one worked example, so the rule can be seen applied. Third, it must name what counts as following the rule and what counts as breaking it, so compliance can be checked. Fourth, it must cite its basis: the directive, the standard, or the public source it rests on. Fifth, it must state where the rule is enforced, by whom or by what check. Sixth, it must record exceptions plainly, with the reason for each. Seventh, it must be teachable, ordered simple to complex along the teaching ladder. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: This database connects through four interfaces. The naming interface gives every new database, record, and generator its identifier from the same rules, so no two things share an identifier. The provenance interface records the source, the date, and the author of every record, so attribution stays straight. The registry interface lists every database and website with its slug, its record kind, its identifier prefix, and its count, so the Master AI can navigate the whole network from one list. The verification interface exposes the checks: completeness, duplication, and identifier rules, each runnable against any database. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every rule they enforce is written in this database, not hidden in code. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Provenance Rules, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Provenance Rules inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

JAH-JUSKS-000006 · Signature Stamps

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Signature Stamps inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Outside the scope are the subject contents of the individual databases, which belong to their own records, and the operating systems of the network, which are not recorded here as working systems. This database records the rules and the map; it does not duplicate the territory. Requirements, as design requirements: Every record in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must state the rule or map exactly, in plain words, with every technical term defined on first use. Second, it must give at least one worked example, so the rule can be seen applied. Third, it must name what counts as following the rule and what counts as breaking it, so compliance can be checked. Fourth, it must cite its basis: the directive, the standard, or the public source it rests on. Fifth, it must state where the rule is enforced, by whom or by what check. Sixth, it must record exceptions plainly, with the reason for each. Seventh, it must be teachable, ordered simple to complex along the teaching ladder. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: This database connects through four interfaces. The naming interface gives every new database, record, and generator its identifier from the same rules, so no two things share an identifier. The provenance interface records the source, the date, and the author of every record, so attribution stays straight. The registry interface lists every database and website with its slug, its record kind, its identifier prefix, and its count, so the Master AI can navigate the whole network from one list. The verification interface exposes the checks: completeness, duplication, and identifier rules, each runnable against any database. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every rule they enforce is written in this database, not hidden in code. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Database Standards, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Database Standards inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

Verification path: Re-read the catalog item #40 and the cross-check row for it; confirm the slug and prefix are the pair's own; run this record's demo and confirm Deterministic: MATCH; confirm the design-study label, the maturity label, and the implementation status appear in the record; confirm the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions appear in plain words; confirm every cited source returns HTTP 200.

JAH-JUSKS-000010 · Record Completeness Rules

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Record Completeness Rules inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Outside the scope are the subject contents of the individual databases, which belong to their own records, and the operating systems of the network, which are not recorded here as working systems. This database records the rules and the map; it does not duplicate the territory. Requirements, as design requirements: Every record in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must state the rule or map exactly, in plain words, with every technical term defined on first use. Second, it must give at least one worked example, so the rule can be seen applied. Third, it must name what counts as following the rule and what counts as breaking it, so compliance can be checked. Fourth, it must cite its basis: the directive, the standard, or the public source it rests on. Fifth, it must state where the rule is enforced, by whom or by what check. Sixth, it must record exceptions plainly, with the reason for each. Seventh, it must be teachable, ordered simple to complex along the teaching ladder. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: This database connects through four interfaces. The naming interface gives every new database, record, and generator its identifier from the same rules, so no two things share an identifier. The provenance interface records the source, the date, and the author of every record, so attribution stays straight. The registry interface lists every database and website with its slug, its record kind, its identifier prefix, and its count, so the Master AI can navigate the whole network from one list. The verification interface exposes the checks: completeness, duplication, and identifier rules, each runnable against any database. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every rule they enforce is written in this database, not hidden in code. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Signature Editions, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Signature Editions inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

Signature Public Editions: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge

JAH-JUSKS-000014 · Public Editions

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Public Editions inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Public Editions as one working part of the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Its working angle is outputs. Scope, as a design: The scope covers the identity and architecture of the network itself: Signature identity, canonical record rules, the JAH architecture overview, naming and identifiers, provenance rules, Signature stamps, the Master AI directive, network navigation, database standards, record completeness rules, the teaching ladder, mirror rules, Signature editions, public editions, lineage and attribution, the JCC code system, march-to-a-million markers, the artificial intelligence of each database, generator standards, verification rules, accessibility rules, free-forever rules, governance records, and the Signature knowledge map. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the individual databases, which belong to their own records, and the operating systems of the network, which are not recorded here as working systems. This database records the rules and the map; it does not duplicate the territory. Requirements, as design requirements: Every record in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must state the rule or map exactly, in plain words, with every technical term defined on first use. Second, it must give at least one worked example, so the rule can be seen applied. Third, it must name what counts as following the rule and what counts as breaking it, so compliance can be checked. Fourth, it must cite its basis: the directive, the standard, or the public source it rests on. Fifth, it must state where the rule is enforced, by whom or by what check. Sixth, it must record exceptions plainly, with the reason for each. Seventh, it must be teachable, ordered simple to complex along the teaching ladder. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: This database connects through four interfaces. The naming interface gives every new database, record, and generator its identifier from the same rules, so no two things share an identifier. The provenance interface records the source, the date, and the author of every record, so attribution stays straight. The registry interface lists every database and website with its slug, its record kind, its identifier prefix, and its count, so the Master AI can navigate the whole network from one list. The verification interface exposes the checks: completeness, duplication, and identifier rules, each runnable against any database. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every rule they enforce is written in this database, not hidden in code. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for March-to-a-Million Markers, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach March-to-a-Million Markers inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

Signature AI Per Database: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge

JAH-JUSKS-000018 · AI Per Database

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for AI Per Database inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for AI Per Database as one working part of the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Its working angle is measurement. Its checks are run by execution, not by reading: the demo recomputes the record's figures twice and compares digests, the program reruns the same computation, and any mismatch stops the step. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers the identity and architecture of the network itself: Signature identity, canonical record rules, the JAH architecture overview, naming and identifiers, provenance rules, Signature stamps, the Master AI directive, network navigation, database standards, record completeness rules, the teaching ladder, mirror rules, Signature editions, public editions, lineage and attribution, the JCC code system, march-to-a-million markers, the artificial intelligence of each database, generator standards, verification rules, accessibility rules, free-forever rules, governance records, and the Signature knowledge map. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the individual databases, which belong to their own records, and the operating systems of the network, which are not recorded here as working systems. This database records the rules and the map; it does not duplicate the territory. Requirements, as design requirements: Every record in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must state the rule or map exactly, in plain words, with every technical term defined on first use. Second, it must give at least one worked example, so the rule can be seen applied. Third, it must name what counts as following the rule and what counts as breaking it, so compliance can be checked. Fourth, it must cite its basis: the directive, the standard, or the public source it rests on. Fifth, it must state where the rule is enforced, by whom or by what check. Sixth, it must record exceptions plainly, with the reason for each. Seventh, it must be teachable, ordered simple to complex along the teaching ladder. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: This database connects through four interfaces. The naming interface gives every new database, record, and generator its identifier from the same rules, so no two things share an identifier. The provenance interface records the source, the date, and the author of every record, so attribution stays straight. The registry interface lists every database and website with its slug, its record kind, its identifier prefix, and its count, so the Master AI can navigate the whole network from one list. The verification interface exposes the checks: completeness, duplication, and identifier rules, each runnable against any database. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every rule they enforce is written in this database, not hidden in code. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Accessibility Rules, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Accessibility Rules inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

Signature Free-Forever Rules: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge

JAH-JUSKS-000022 · Free-Forever Rules

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Free-Forever Rules inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

6. Generator

Generator: seeded boundless generator, prefix JAH-JUSKS-GEN-, hard stop 1,000,000,000 outputs per file, throttled to 100 per call. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Each record carries the same boundless generator: it makes full records on demand; generated content is labeled simulated for weeding and double-checked.

7. AI

The AI of this database is JAH Universal Signature Librarian (Signature). It explains this database in plain, conversational language, answers questions about any of the 24 records, and can create an AI assistant exactly like itself. It answers from this database's own data only, and says plainly when a question goes beyond it.

  • ai-thing.json — the per-database AI spec (the database's own AI, JAH Universal Signature Librarian (Signature)).
  • ai-discussion.json — the discussion AI spec, built to discuss The Signature JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database with a visitor, record by record.

Both are free downloads, free forever, no sign-in.

8. Demo

Every record in this database carries a working demo program and a boundless generator. The demo for this website is demo.js — run it with node demo.js. It searches the edition's real 24 records and runs a deterministic generator demo (seed 7) over them. The run output is saved in demo-test.txt and shown below, exactly as executed — this demo actually ran.

DATABASE: jah-universal-signature-knowledge-signature
EDITION: signature
RECORDS: 24
SEARCH "signature": 24 hit(s)
  - JAH-JUSKS-000001 :: Signature Signature Identity: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge
  - JAH-JUSKS-000002 :: Signature Canonical Record Rules: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge
  - JAH-JUSKS-000003 :: Signature JAH Architecture Overview: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge
  - JAH-JUSKS-000004 :: Signature Naming and Identifiers: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge
  - JAH-JUSKS-000005 :: Signature Provenance Rules: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge
SEARCH "" (all): 24 record(s)
GENERATOR DEMO (seed 7, 5 samples):
  GEN-1: Signature Universal
  GEN-2: Records Code
  GEN-3: Teaching Network
  GEN-4: Standards Naming
  GEN-5: Ladder System
TOTAL TITLE CHARS: 1549
TITLE CHECKSUM: 3025813749
DEMO OK

Record demos: each of the 24 records carries its own demo program in the database (Python), with an expected output recorded alongside it; each per-file website re-runs its record's demo.
